1.46: 命とモビリティを結び付けたい。AIエージェントによる業務の連
1.47: 携・自動化を物流・商業・エネルギーへ広げ、車両・サービス・
1.48: 街を一体で実装することで、自動運転だからこそ実現できる利便
1.49: 性と新しいサービスを生み出す。

1.51: 実装する
1.52: 運行・エネルギー補充・整備拠点を組み合わせ、車両稼働率と事業採算の成立条件を捉える。

3.38: 運行を支える拠点の成立条件と、将来事業への接続課題
3.39: ①サービス拠点の整備と、技術転換を織り込んだ事業継続
3.40: 自動運転サービスには、駐車・充電・整備・清掃を担う拠点が必要となる。運行と拠点の予約・作業・電力制御を接続し、街の電力・熱需要との
3.41: 共用で採算を検証する。将来のeVTOL・水素式への展開も見据え、初期設備への投資と事業継続の条件を揃える。
3.42: ②本命となる普及機へのパワートレイン供給継続・商権の維持
3.43: 航空機メーカーの将来機体の仕様・供給先が固まる前に、要求性能・採用条件・開発日程を共有する。電池式への対応にとどまることで、普及期
3.44: の供給機会を失うリスクを見極める。拠点の立上げと並行し、水素式パワートレインへの参画条件を詰める。
3.45: ③拠点の充電と街の分散電源を通じた、定置での技術蓄積
3.46: 自動運転車の充電と街の電力・熱需要を基に、GTFC＋HTSの定置利用を検討する。HTSを発電機として用い、発電・熱利用・水素運用・EMSの
3.47: 実績を蓄積。PV・SOFCとの役割と採算を確かめ、航空用へ生かせる技術と追加開発・認証の範囲を見極める。
3.48: ④今からの協業検討と、初期投資の手戻り防止
3.49: CNエネルギー供給・拠点事業を基に、設備事業者との共同事業・開発、技術共有の条件を検討する。将来機体の要求と技術の成熟度を、設備・
3.50: システムの接続条件と段階投資へ反映。普及機の供給先選定に間に合う検証計画を組み、設備の作り直し・二重投資を防ぐ。
3.51: ※ 航空用供給・搭載、設備事業者との共同事業は将来構想。技術転用・採用条件を検証し、初期サービスは利用可能な設備で先行する。
3.52: 運行を支える拠点開発を初期から進め、航空用供給は関係部門との並行検討課題として扱う。
3.53: 設備・協業の成立条件を確認し、利用可能な設備での初期導入と将来拡張を両立する。

7.46: AI化の移行条件：人の修正率・予約成立率・例外対応負荷を比較し、合意した業務から自動化する。
7.47: 介護・学童・交流を予約・運行・地域サービスの共通基盤で支え、利用機会と運営負荷を改善する。
7.48: 人の承認と判断履歴を残し、実績で効果を確認できた業務からAI支援・自動化の範囲を広げる。

8.34: 定後に実装。
8.35: マイルストーン：成果物／移行条件／判断する関係者
8.36: 住民・現場との対話から、介護・学童・交流で解決する課題、対象地域・利用者、需要の根拠を整理する。 G1 対象合意
8.37: 自治体・提供者・企画が利用場面・評価項目・協議窓口を合意し、現場で検証できるシナリオの設計へ進む。
8.38: アプリ・電話の利用手順とP7の業務を具体化し、共通機能・個別対応・予約変更・引渡し・精算の条件を定める。 G2 要件合意
8.39: 企画・開発・運用・事業者が責任分担・優先順位・見積・費用負担を合意し、初期導入と後続対応の範囲を確定する。
8.40: 予約から提供・精算まで通しで試験し、遅延・取消・障害・引渡し例外の対応と停止・再開の手順を訓練する。 G3 導入判断
8.41: 導入責任者と現場が重大な未解決事項、担当者・連絡先・復旧手順の実効性を確認し、提供範囲を定めて限定導入する。
8.42: 予約成立・利用完了・再利用、待ち時間・対応工数・地域別収支を目標と比較し、未充足需要と負荷の原因を確認する。 G4 継続判断
8.43: 自治体・事業者・企画が提供時間・職員配置・機能の改善を決め、有人対応の実績からAI化範囲と追加投資を判断する。
8.44: 地域の運用条件を導入手順に反映し、外部接続の仕様・契約・保守、追加地域の原価と提供責任を整理する。 G5 展開判断
8.45: 事業・開発・保守責任者が接続・復旧試験、料金・採算と支援体制を確認し、接続先と合意して外部提供・地域追加へ進む。
8.46: 進行管理：課題・担当・期限を記録。変更の費用・日程・運用影響を審査し、未達条件は再検証する。
8.47: 地域で検証した業務と機能を共通PaaSへ展開し、予約・配車・エネマネをつなぐ運用を整える。
8.48: 待機・補充・清掃・日常点検の拠点確保を並行し、サービス需要と設備能力を合わせて拡張する。

9.16: 社員が利用時間・返却先を予約。       利用目的と電力提供実績を記録。       期限内に返却。私用分を個人精算。

4.34: lost acknowledgement → UNKNOWN → query and reconcile; never duplicate a side effect. Revision conflict → refresh terms; stale evidence → block offer or action with reason.

17.28: Predicate + persistence + hysteresis                 Qualified seat + ACK + lease                  Reopen on contradictory evidence
17.29: P0       Collision / fire / SOS / failed fallback. Immediate escalation, no debounce.
17.30: P1    ODD exit / MRM / critical fault / unknown active state. Hold capacity, urgent response.
17.31: P2      Assistance / obstruction / boarding failure. Assess exposure and escalate if worsening.
17.32: P3       Noncritical service or diagnostic issue. Schedule work under explicit restrictions.

17.36: deadlines. Snooze needs reason/expiry and cannot suppress P0. Missing/invalid data raise their own event.

26.1: AUTONOMOUS MOBILITY SERVICE / SYSTEM PLANNING — INITIAL CONCEPT
26.2: Replenishment and departure readiness
26.3: The operating plan reserves replenishment capacity within a vehicle’s duty cycle. Daily energy sufficiency and on-time departure are
26.4: separate acceptance conditions.
26.5: 19.1 Vehicle cycle with finite replenishment resources
26.6: Arrive              Queue                 Replenish             Turnaround            Release
26.7: Observed energy  →  Bay / connector   →  Power or fuel    →  Clean / inspect   →  Energy + tasks
26.8: Arrival time                  Staff eligibility              Actual delivery            Required evidence        Next departure
26.9: Departure constraint
26.10: t_arrival + t_queue + t_replenish + t_required_work ≤ t_departure
26.11: Required work includes passenger / cargo handling, cleaning and inspection as applicable.
26.12: Overlap only approved tasks. Recheck route energy and all next departure / delivery commitments.
26.13: 19.2 Electrical and fuel branches use distinct units
26.14: Electrical charging                                          Fuel / hydrogen replenishment
26.15: E_depart = E_arrive + η_ch ∫ P_AC(t) dt − E_aux                      m_depart = m_arrive + m_delivered − m_consumed
26.16: E_next_trip + E_reserve ≤ E_depart ≤ E_usable,max                   m_next_trip + m_reserve ≤ m_depart ≤ m_usable,max
26.17: P_AC in kW, time in hours, energy in kWh.                       Mass in kg, flow in kg/min, duration in minutes.
26.18: Use charger / battery limits and charging efficiency.               Bound usable stock, dispenser flow and turnaround.
26.19: Energy plan ACK does not prove delivered energy.                     Fuel-to-energy conversion needs an explicit model.
26.20: SITE LIMITS AT EVERY TIME INTERVAL
26.21: Σ P_charge,i(t) ≤ P_site,available(t)  and  occupied connectors ≤ serviceable connectors
26.22: P_site,available reflects building load, generation, storage and connection limits at the same boundary.
26.23: Fuel stock remains non-negative after deliveries and withdrawals. One hub can contain several resources.
26.24: 19.3 Shortage propagation and recovery

28.2: Operating feasibility and verification evidence

29.1: AUTONOMOUS MOBILITY SERVICE / SYSTEM PLANNING — INITIAL CONCEPT
29.2: Shared rides: route feasibility and passenger outcomes
29.3: 04. Sharing can improve passenger output per vehicle-hour when compatible journeys fit the same route. Seat occupancy alone does not
29.4: prove better service or contribution.
29.5: 22.1 Request compatibility and guarded route insertion
29.6: Eligible requests                       Candidate insertion                 Commit and observe
29.7: Pickup / arrival windows                         Insert pickup before drop-off                 Hold seats and route revision
29.8: Party size and accessible space    →   Recompute every downstream ETA   →    Confirm accepted terms per party
29.9: Sharing consent and fare terms              Check capacity on every segment             Observe boarding / alighting
29.10: Maximum accepted ride time                Keep existing passenger promises            Keep each intent and outcome
29.11: 22.2 Segment capacity and individual service constraints
29.12: Capacity along the route
29.13: For each segment s: Σ_i onboard(i,s) × partySize_i ≤ seats_usable(s)
29.14: Check accessible positions and luggage space separately under the vehicle configuration.
29.15: A pooled route is one vehicle mission with several passenger bookings. Count its distance once.
29.16: Passenger time constraints                                  Insertion and consent rules
29.17: earliestPickup_i ≤ pickup_i ≤ latestPickup_i                        Recheck every onboard and committed passenger.
29.18: arrival_i ≤ latestArrival_i                                              Protect accepted windows and access requirements.
29.19: rideTime_i − directTime_i ≤ detourAllowance_i                         Offer sharing price and allowed detour explicitly.
29.20: Direct time uses the same traffic / time basis.                    An infeasible insertion returns a binding reason.
29.21: Include boarding, alighting and intermediate dwell.                No-show or cancellation triggers reconciliation.
29.22: 22.3 Revenue gain, service loss and the comparison basis
29.23: Passenger output                    Net contribution                     Comparable alternatives
29.24: Passenger journeys / vehicle-hour             Recognized fares after discounts            Same underlying passenger intents
29.25: Passenger-km / vehicle-km      →    Less route, energy and support cost  →   Same fleet, hubs and faults
29.26: Seat-km used / available seat-km               Include added dwell and recovery               Single-party vs shared operation
29.27: Wait, detour and missed pickups              Route cost allocated only once                 Rejected and displaced trips visible
29.28: 22.4 Failure cases and release evidence
29.29: Stress cases
29.30: Mismatched OD / low overlap; full accessible space; delayed boarding; no-show after insertion;
29.31: traffic delay; cancellation while onboard; vehicle loss with several parties needing recovery.
29.32: Stop adding riders when an accepted passenger limit or response-capacity constraint would fail.
29.33: Evidence: route revision, ordered stops, segment occupancy, accepted detour / fare, actual passenger times and booking-level outcomes.
29.34: Show total contribution and passenger fulfillment together. Higher occupancy can coexist with fewer completed journeys. Agree
29.35: thresholds and operating authority before pilot release.

30.1: AUTONOMOUS MOBILITY SERVICE / SYSTEM PLANNING — INITIAL CONCEPT
30.2: Passenger–freight sharing: capacity and net contribution
30.3: 05. Cargo uses compatible spare route and vehicle capacity. Incremental freight revenue must cover handling and detour costs while
30.4: preserving passenger service commitments.
30.5: 23.1 Cargo eligibility, route insertion and custody
30.6: Shipment request                          Joint feasibility                            Verified delivery
30.7: Origin / destination and windows             Passenger windows remain binding             Link shipment to route revision
30.8: Mass, volume and compartment    →   Check payload, space and access   →    Record pickup / custody handovers
30.9: needs                                           Insert loading / unloading dwell                 Delivery or failed-attempt proof
30.10: Handling time and handover contact           Reserve staff and loading space           Own return, loss and damage cases
30.11: Permitted goods / service policy
30.12: 23.2 Capacity is a vector at each route segment
30.13: Independent constraints
30.14: Passenger seats and accessible positions remain within their configured limits.
30.15: Σ_j cargoVolume_j,s ≤ usableCargoVolume_s  and   Σ_j onboardMass_j,s ≤ payloadLimit_s
30.16: Onboard mass includes people, luggage and cargo under the same payload boundary.
30.17: Check segregation, securement, door access and passenger comfort before accepting a job.
30.18: Time and recovery constraint                                Service policy and authority
30.19: Route time = travel + passenger dwell                           Use an approved vehicle / goods combination.
30.20: + cargo handling + required turnaround.                           Confirm transport, insurance and partner eligibility.
30.21: Recompute every pickup, arrival and delivery ETA.                    Assign loading, custody and exception owners.
30.22: Cargo waiting / failed handover consumes capacity.                   Decline cargo that breaks passenger commitments.
30.23: Protect passenger recovery and energy reserve.                     Apply return / storage rules after failed delivery.
30.24: 23.3 Four operating conditions on the same demand basis
30.25: Single-party                Shared rides                Passenger + cargo          Combined
30.26: No pooling / cargo                Pooling only                   Cargo only                       Pooling + cargo
30.27: Economic comparison
30.28: Δ contribution = Δ passenger revenue + freight revenue − Δ total attributable operating cost
30.29: Passenger revenue reflects discounts and displaced / unserved trips. Do not subtract that loss twice.
30.30: Include handling, detours, energy, cleaning, support, returns / claims and app cost. Count route cost once.
30.31: 23.4 Outcome and failure evidence
30.32: Report passenger fulfillment / delay and freight delivery / lateness separately, plus vehicle-hours, payload / volume limits, dwell and total
30.33: contribution. Test full payload, long unloading, absent recipient, incompatible goods, cancellation and vehicle loss. Keep passenger and
30.34: shipment identities within one shared mission. Hold effects 01–03 constant across the four conditions, then test interactions.

1.1: PORTFOLIO
1.2: Autonomous Mobility Business Execution Architecture
1.3: ― Driving autonomous mobility business development through AD/ADAS system foundation ―
1.4: Autonomous Mobility Business Definition
1.5: This architecture is defined as a control system where KPI, system behavior, and business outcomes are directly
1.6: connected. Each KPI is traced to the system behavior that moves it and to its effect on revenue, cost, and return.
1.7: The objective is to build an AI-enabled autonomous mobility business architecture that improves utilization, load
1.8: factor, and unit economics.
1.9: This is achieved by integrating mobility, logistics, energy, and urban services through a common operating
1.10: architecture, while structurally improving:
1.11: •   utilization
1.12: •   load factor
1.13: •   unit economics
1.14: This proposal defines a business architecture that makes this system executable, economically grounded, and
1.15: deployable.
1.16: Autonomous Mobility Business Execution
1.17: This proposal defines a phased development and deployment plan for autonomous mobility business execution.
1.18: Today’s mobility systems fail to scale because demand, fleet, energy, and operations are still managed separately.
1.19: This architecture integrates these elements into a unified autonomous mobility business structure, using autonomy
1.20: as the foundation and AI as the means to operate autonomous mobility at business scale. System performance
1.21: directly translates into revenue through utilization, load factor, and energy efficiency:
1.22: • Demand creation synchronized with dispatch
1.23: • Utilization maximization through modular vehicle operation
1.24: • Load factor optimization through dynamic vehicle composition
1.25: • Energy cost reduction through on-site hydrogen production
1.26: • Real-time operational control through distributed node coordination and AI-enabled optimization
1.27: Autonomous Mobility Business Architecture
1.28: Objective: To define an executable autonomous mobility business structure where AI-driven control logic is applied
1.29: through autonomous driving to integrate demand, operations, vehicles, and infrastructure.
1.30: This architecture shifts mobility from fragmented services into a unified operating system where utilization, load
1.31: factor, and energy efficiency are structurally optimized.
1.32: AI is applied as controlled logic across four layers.
1.33: 1. Demand Orchestration
1.34: 2030: Coordinated AI Assistant
1.35: A calendar-based interface connects user schedules with service availability, enabling demand generation
1.36: through matching and reservation-linked mobility.
1.37: 2035: Trusted AI Partner
1.38: A 4D digital platform integrated with map infrastructure synchronizes city-state and user behavior in real
1.39: time, enabling continuous demand generation and capture to maximize load factor.
1.40: 2. Operation Control
1.41: 2030: Integrated Control
1.42: Energy management and dispatch are coordinated through decision logic that can later be augmented by
1.43: AI, enabling stable and efficient operations under constrained conditions.
1.44: 2035: Synchronized Control
1.45: Real-time synchronization of in-car and out-car hydrogen and electricity flows creates the control
1.46: foundation for AI-assisted optimization, while structurally improving business viability.
1.47: 3. Fleet Vehicle
1.48: 2030: Swap Carrier
1.49: A modular vehicle structure enables continuous operation through container exchange, maximizing
1.50: utilization.
1.51: 2035: Composed Carrier
1.52: Vehicles dynamically couple and reconfigure during operation, enabling real-time redistribution of
1.53: passengers and cargo to maximize load factor.
1.54: 4. Distributed Infrastructure
1.55: 2030: Urban Node
1.56: Distributed hubs provide localized energy and compute support, enabling operational independence and
1.57: cost efficiency.
1.58: 2035: Node Mesh
1.59: Distributed infrastructure is synchronized as a network, enabling real-time coordination between vehicles,
1.60: energy, and computation.
1.61: This architecture enables a system where mobility, energy, computing, and AI-based decision-making operate as a
1.62: unified layer, enabling scalable autonomous mobility business operation.
1.63: Satofumi Goto
----PAGE 2: 48 text rows----
2.1: AI-Enabled Urban Mobility Roadmap
2.2: Autonomous mobility becomes deployable only when a production-ready driving stack, closed-loop learning, auditable
2.3: safety, fleet operations and service economics are developed as one system. This roadmap first establishes that industrial
2.4: baseline, then extends AI control from vehicles to distributed infrastructure and city-scale operations.
2.5: Industry Baseline & Capability Build-Up

2.10: Real-event replay, scenario variation, closed-course ODD-specific scenario coverage, acceptance
2.11: Simulation / V&V   and public-road tests, synthetic simulation and        criteria, critical-event recall, safety evidence and
2.12: automated regression.                                validation gates.
2.13: ODD safety cases, independent evidence,          Claims, evidence, escalation, incident response,  Safety / Remote                     fallback/MRM, remote monitoring and contextual    cybersecurity, rollback, audit trail and remote-  Support                     assistance with clear authority boundaries.          support limits.
2.14: Integrated dispatch, maintenance, charging,       Connect the driving stack with fleet and service  Fleet / Service                       incident management, customer support and         control; manage SLA, availability, intervention,  Operations                    feedback from daily autonomous operations.       energy and cost/hour.
2.15: Reusable software/hardware platforms, lower-cost  Track utilization, launch lead time, ODD rollout

2.17: multi-vehicle deployment.                           use-case-specific functions.
2.18: The industrial baseline combines a production-ready driver, closed-loop learning, scenario-based V&V, auditable safety,
2.19: remote support, fleet operations and repeatable ODD deployment. The roadmap establishes these capabilities first, then
2.20: differentiates through city data, infrastructure coordination, demand orchestration and business optimization.
2.21: AI-Controlled Mobility System
2.22: This section defines how AI functions as control logic across layers and is executed through autonomous driving
2.23: systems. AI is applied differently at each layer — from demand prediction to system-wide coordination — but all
2.24: functions are integrated through a unified control structure that enables real-time execution at city scale.
2.25: Layer                Cloud                  Distributed Infrastructure                Fleet Vehicle

2.36: ODD / DDT as Revenue Driver
2.37: ODD / DDT transition defines idle time and directly determines utilization and revenue structure.
2.38: DDT Loss = (1-TSR) x Recovery time + TOT, Revenue = OAR x UAC x (1÷(Trip+(1-TSR) x Recovery + TOT) x FLC x Load

3.60: Validated policies, models and operating rules move downward; evidence, uncertainty, incidents and business KPIs
3.61: move upward. Safety authority remains local, while learning and optimization remain globally governed, measurable and
3.62: auditable.
3.63: Across these three layers, the operating cycle remains Observe → Understand → Learn → Evaluate → Execute →
3.64: Observe Again. Real-world signals are interpreted and protected at the edge; governed data is federated and converted
3.65: into validated improvements; approved policies and models are then deployed back to vehicles, RSUs, infrastructure and
3.66: urban operations. Evidence and learning move upward, while only validated policies and control updates move
3.67: downward. The architecture scales from coordinated assistance to cross-domain orchestration across mobility, logistics,
3.68: energy and public services, while preserving human accountability, fallback and rollback. Quantum computing, sensing
3.69: and quantum-enhanced perception are introduced as evidence-led accelerators—not assumptions—and must
3.70: demonstrate measurable gains in safety, resilience, service quality, capacity or lifecycle economics before deployment.
3.71: The result is a practical path from isolated AI functions to a trusted, continuously improving urban operating system.

5.1: System Design for Mobility Feasibility
5.2: This page defines scenario design targets for Energy Efficiency = 0.58 (=58%), Utilization = 0.75 (=18h/day ÷ 24h/day), and Load
5.3: Factor = 0.70 (=2.8pax ÷ 4pax). They are not proven outcomes; each target requires metered validation through integrated system
5.4: architecture.
5.5: The design is structured across energy supply [kWh/day], vehicle dynamics and response [ms, cm, Hz], and control and computation
5.6: [ms, Hz]. Net traction demand is 2,080.5kWh/day (=28.5kWh/trip × 73trips/node). At 60% electrical efficiency, required fuel-LHV input is
5.7: 3,467.5kWh/day and the hydrogen-equivalent requirement is 104.1kg/day (=3,467.5 ÷ 33.3). Annual PV energy can exceed annual net
5.8: demand, but an annual ratio does not prove 24-hour supply without power, storage, loss, and weather modelling.
5.9: Vehicle and control design ensure "motion sickness free" operation, defined by control latency ≤20ms, decision latency ≤100ms,
5.10: positioning error ≤10cm, and vibration ≤0.3Hz. These are maintained through superconductivity enabled by liquid hydrogen cooling and
5.11: real-time control via HPC operating at 100Hz (=1 ÷ 0.01sec). The HPC value is a design target and does not by itself prove continuous
5.12: satisfaction or ODD expansion.

6.1: Feasibility Conditions for Autonomous Mobility System
6.2: This page does not present financial outcomes. It defines scenario conditions that must be independently validated. Energy Efficiency
6.3: = 0.58, Utilization = 0.75, and Load Factor = 0.70 are design targets, not derived facts. Feasibility depends on energy supply
6.4: [kWh/day], available power [kW], storage duration [h], vehicle response [ms], control accuracy [cm], ODD evidence, and commercial
6.5: constraints.
6.6: Powertrain alternatives must be compared on duty-cycle efficiency, degradation, refuelling/charging downtime, mass, packaging,
6.7: infrastructure, and total cost. Direct-hydrogen PEM fuel cells are not excluded by a 22% efficiency assumption; DOE lists
6.8: approximately 60% electrical efficiency for direct-H2 PEM. MS-SOFC remains the selected base-case candidate for this concept,
6.9: subject to full TCO, durability, start-up, emissions, and ODD validation.
6.10: Node operation requires 2,080.5kWh/day of net traction energy. At 60% efficiency the fuel-LHV requirement is 3,467.5kWh/day,
6.11: equivalent to 104.1kg-H2/day. PV generation of 1,402,500kWh/year gives an annual energy ratio of approximately 1.85, but continuous
6.12: operation still requires an hourly supply-demand, storage, conversion-loss, and weather model.
6.13: "Motion sickness free" refers to a condition where control latency ≤20ms, decision latency ≤100ms, positioning error ≤10cm, and
6.14: vibration ≤0.3Hz are simultaneously satisfied. This condition determines user acceptance and directly enables Utilization = 0.75
6.15: (=18h/day ÷ 24h/day). HPC maintains control at an execution rate of 100Hz (=1 ÷ 0.01sec), ensuring continuous satisfaction of
6.16: response constraints. The definition is retained as the design requirement; compliance must be demonstrated by test evidence.

7.1: Proposed Responsibility Structure for Autonomous Mobility System
7.2: This is a proposed RACI hypothesis for discussion, not a statement of current accountability of any specific company or consortium.
7.3: Final responsibility must be confirmed by corporate governance, contracts, permits, product-liability allocation, operational rules, and
7.4: the approved safety case.
7.5: Continuous operation depends on explicit ownership across orchestration, fleet operation, vehicle/system safety, node execution,
7.6: energy supply, commercial settlement, and finance. A method such as model-based development is not an accountable legal entity;
7.7: each task must be assigned to an organization or contracted operator.

9.1: Autonomous Mobility Business Control Structure
9.2: System conditions define the feasibility of control and set operational boundaries. Values below are phase targets or scenario
9.3: assumptions; they are not externally verified outcomes. Minimum gates on P1 and phase targets here are different by design and must
9.4: be labelled separately.
9.5: Revenue uses unit-consistent formulas: annual passenger revenue = Nodes × Trips/day per node × Seats × Load factor ×
9.6: Blended fare × 365.   Break-even trips/day cannot be calculated without a defined annual controllable-cost allocation.
9.7: P&L  Layer        Control item                      Decision logic                   2030          2035                                                                                                        impact
9.8: Demand Demand generation      Requests ÷ potential demand                    ≥0.50          ≥0.70            Trips
9.9: Conversion              Bookings ÷ requests                            ≥0.23          ≥0.35            Trips
9.10: Matching               Matched ÷ requests                            ≥0.90          ≥0.95            Trips
9.11: Trip realization           Executed ÷ matched                            ≥0.95          ≥0.95            Trips
9.12: Stabilization            Matched trips + planned demand                 ≥0.80          ≥0.95            Trips
9.13: Time synchronization     Calendar-based demand                        ≥0.60          ≥0.85            Trips
9.14: Service capture          Reservation-based trips ÷ total                   ≥0.50          ≥0.75            Trips
9.15: Demand-cap control     Demand ≤ system capacity                 100%        100%           Trips cap
9.16: Operation Trips                   Executed trips/day per node                   73            140             Trips
9.17: Utilization                Operating time ÷ 24h                           ≥0.75          ≥0.85            Trips
9.18: Dispatch success         Successful dispatch ÷ requests                  ≥0.95          ≥0.99            Trips
9.19: Supply-body cycle         Active body ÷ total cycle                         ≥0.80          ≥0.95             Utilization
9.20: Waiting time            Passenger request-to-pickup                   ≤360sec       ≤180sec         Trips
9.21: Turnaround                 Idle + service time                           ≤25min        ≤10min           Utilization
9.22: Queue control              Total wait ÷ requests                          ≤300sec       ≤120sec        Conversion
9.23: Optimization            Served ÷ queued                               ≥0.90          ≥0.98            Trips
9.24: Scheduled continuity      Continuous-operation ratio                      ≥0.80          ≥0.95             Utilization

9.27: Stability                   Deviation ÷ target                       ≤5%        ≤5%            Safety

13.1: IRR and Cash Flow Structure
13.2: This page adds externally sold distributed-HPC and quantum capacity to the reconciled base cash flow. Node plan 4/8/12/20/28/36;
13.3: HPC CAPEX of ¥5B/¥45B implies ¥8.33M per unit and 150 H100-equivalents/node; paid use rises 35% to 55% at ¥700 to ¥600 per
13.4: accelerator-hour (1.2kW, PUE 1.15, ¥15/kWh, service 15% of revenue). One quantum module per three-node mesh (1/2/4/6/9/12)
13.5: costs ¥1.0B (7y) and sells dedicated hours at 20% to 40% paid use and ¥450,000 to ¥375,000/h (USD 3,000 to 2,500 at ¥150),
13.6: with 25kW, upkeep at 10% of CAPEX and service at 15%. Internal mobility compute is excluded; the case is pre-tax and unlevered.
13.7: IRR is the discount rate that sets NPV to zero. Project payback is the first year in which cumulative nominal FCF becomes non-
13.8: negative. Discounted payback is assessed separately. Node payback cannot be calculated because node-level revenue, OPEX,
13.9: CAPEX, tax, and renewal cash flow are not separately specified.

14.1: Financial Interpretation & KPI Linkage
14.2: The displayed case adds separately priced distributed-HPC and quantum-hour services, excluding internal mobility compute. 2035
14.3: HPC/quantum revenue is ¥15.610B/¥15.768B; incremental EBITDA ¥12.730B/¥12.164B. Annual project FCF is positive from 2034, but
14.4: cumulative nominal FCF remains ▲¥228.358B at the end of 2035. Nominal and discounted payback are not achieved; project IRR is
14.5: -36.49% and NPV at 8% is ▲¥196.235B (without quantum: -77.97% and ▲¥205.5B). AV-AI training gains from quantum: to verify.
14.6: The largest unresolved drivers are contracted HPC capacity, paid GPU utilization, accelerator-hour price, node delivery cost, fleet
14.7: OPEX, hydrogen volume/cost, platform pricing, depreciation-heavy investment, working capital, tax, and renewal CAPEX. KPI
14.8: improvements affect finance only through explicit volume, price, cost, and investment equations.
14.9: Node Deployment Plan

14.12: By 2032 (scenario)               12                 Fleet synchronization
14.13: By 2035                       36                  City-scale operation
14.14: KPI and Financial Equation
14.15: KPI                  Financial impact                               Required equation
14.16: Utilization              Revenue increase               available vehicle-hours × monetized trips/hour
14.17: Fleet idle ratio         OPEX/CAPEX efficiency         idle hours × avoidable fleet cost
14.18: Matching rate              Trips increase                 requests × matching × dispatch × realization
14.19: Dispatch success        Revenue stability            matched trips × successful dispatch
14.20: Energy cost/trip          Margin                      energy input × unit cost ÷ trips
14.21: Node/HPC utilization    CAPEX efficiency              paid GPU-hours ÷ available external GPU-hours
14.22: GPU-hour price         Compute revenue              contracted HPC revenue ÷ paid GPU-hours
14.23: Trips/day              Revenue scale                     fleet × available hours × trips/hour
14.24: Load factor             Revenue efficiency            occupied passenger-km ÷ available seat-km
14.25: EBITDA margin         Cash generation            EBITDA ÷ revenue
14.26: Operating margin         Accounting profit               operating profit ÷ revenue

14.44: Investment Decision Logic
14.45: - Autonomous-mobility profitability is dominated by utilization, load, price, energy, fleet OPEX, and infrastructure CAPEX - not labor reduction
14.46: alone.
14.47: - Node deployment may convert parking assets into operational infrastructure only after contract, COGS, utilization, permit, and residual-value
14.48: assumptions are defined.
14.49: - AVP-based infrastructure sharing can support private users and MaaS fleets, but revenue cannot be recognized twice across base fares,
14.50: platform fees, node fees, and settlements.
14.51: - Hydrogen-energy integration can reduce OPEX only if metered end-to-end efficiency, production cost, storage, losses, and uptime support
14.52: the claimed unit cost.
14.53: - Vehicle OEMs, mobility-platform providers, operators..., suppliers, and finance entities require contractually explicit authority and
14.54: accountability; the responsibility structure is proposed, not current fact.
14.55: - At the displayed base, distributed-HPC sales lower project returns versus the no-HPC case and do not achieve nominal payback by 2035;
14.56: contracted capacity, staged investment, lower CAPEX/OPEX, or alternative financing remains necessary.
